Financial Report Source 04

Document 006: 10月进出口数据点评：AI量价分化延续，美欧经贸格局生变

Source PDF: all report pdf/10月进出口数据点评：AI量价分化延续，美欧经贸格局生变.pdf
Report source: 国信证券
Date: 2026-11-11
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

事项：
11 月 10日，海关总署发布进出口数据。我国 10 月出口（以美元计价，后同）同比增长 35.2%；进口同比
增长 41.6%，贸易顺差 1052.77亿美元。
评论：
结论：
10月外贸增速维持高位，电子产业链支撑较强，价格因素推升名义增长。出口同比增长 35.2%，较上月小
幅回落，1—10 月累计增长 22.7%；进口同比增长 41.6%，较上月加快 10.6 个百分点，1—10 月累计增长
28.9%。我们测算，集成电路和自动数据处理设备及零部件合计拉动出口增长 13.6个百分点，仍是外贸高
景气度的核心因素。
出口方面，电子产品支撑较强，传统品类表现分化，AI 品类出口增速量价背离值得关注。10 月出口有三
点变化值得注意，第一，电子产业链仍是出口增长的核心支撑，但金额高增主要依靠均价抬升。集成电路、
计算机相关产品出口金额分别增长 137.1%和 114.8%；但集成电路出口数量反而下降 2.5%，隐含均价上涨
143.2%，若后续涨价放缓、同期基数抬升，名义增速或面临回落压力。第二，传统制造出口仍有亮点，但

增长动能有所分化。通用机械、汽车零配件出口分别增长 34.5%和 32.1%，较上月加快；汽车、家电和玩
具则分别增长 37.6%、20.9%和 27.5%，增速有所回落，传统品类的改善尚不均衡。第三，成品油出口同比
保持高增，环比下行，原因在于国内保供扰动短期发运。月初炼厂为补充国内库存暂停部分出口、假期后
恢复安排，导致出口数量环比下降 27.6%。
进口方面，电子与原材料共同支撑高增，价格贡献较大，实物需求表现分化。一方面，电子产品进口保持
高景气，亚洲产业链贸易活跃。集成电路、计算机相关产品进口金额分别增长 78.3%和 261.0%；自韩国、
东盟进口分别增长 110.2%和 42.0%，相关产业链仍是进口增长的重要支撑。另一方面，部分原材料及汽车
进口仍偏弱，需求恢复存在结构性差异。铁矿砂进口金额下降 11.5%，铜矿砂进口数量下降 11.6%；汽车
及零配件进口金额分别下降 38.5%和 41.7%。
往后看，AI 产业链仍将支撑外贸韧性，但价格效应减弱与贸易政策调整，可能推动出口增速回落。此外，

随着中期选举结束、中欧经贸磋商落地，我国与美欧之间的贸易关系将出现变化。中美经贸存在维持阶段
性稳定的空间，科技领域竞争仍可能趋紧。民主党赢得两院后，特朗普推进政策将面临更多国会制约；我
们判断，稳物价、稳经济的诉求可能增加双方维持谈判的动力，但行政端贸易措施仍有反复风险。与此同
时，对华科技限制具有较强的两党共识，先进芯片、半导体设备和 AI 相关出口管制仍可能收紧，电子产
业链将同时面对需求支撑与政策约束。中欧经贸磋商后，混动汽车出口数量约束将限制相关品类增长，对
欧出口面临“政策确定性改善、数量扩张受限”的双重影响。

 出口：高增延续，AI品类量价背离
10 出口同比保持较高增速。出口同比保持较高增速，但 9 月冲高后的环比回落值得关注。10 月出口同比
增长 35.2%，较上月小幅放缓，1—10 月累计增长 22.7%；当月金额环比下降 7.8%。国庆假期及节前后集
中发运可能影响月度节奏。外需仍处扩张区间，新增订单边际降温。10 月全球制造业 PMI 为 52.6，较 9
月的 53.0回落；中国制造业新出口订单指数由 50.0降至 48.7。外需尚有支撑、增量动能有所放缓。
从商品结构上看，电子产品高增更多体现在金额端，数量出现收缩。集成电路、自动数据处理设备及其零
部件出口金额分别增长 137.1%和 114.8%，但集成电路数量同比下降 2.5%，金额与数量走势背离。汽车、
家电和玩具出口增速分别回落至 37.6%、20.9%和 27.5%；通用机械、汽车零配件则加快至 34.5%和 32.1%，
传统制造出口并非全面走弱。汽车出口数量同比仍增长 33.5%。

从国别结构上看，亚洲贸易联系仍较活跃，欧美市场增速有所分化。对韩国出口同比增长 92.4%，较上月
明显加快；对东盟增长 46.3%，虽有放缓，仍保持较高增速。对美国出口增长 32.9%，较上月回落；对欧
盟增长 24.3%，总体变化较小。国别表现与电子产业链景气具有一致性。
成品油同比仍强，国内保供放大了环比发运波动。10月成品油出口金额同比增长 80.3%，数量增长 14.6%、
隐含均价上涨 57.3%；数量环比则下降 27.6%。这主要是因为炼厂为补充国内库存，月初暂停部分成品油
出口。

Document 021: 2014年GPIF改革から考える国内債券の位置づけ ～「金利のある世界」で現行配分は最適か～

Source PDF: all report pdf/2014年GPIF改革から考える国内債券の位置づけ ～「金利のある世界」で現行配分は最適か～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-06
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

Research Report

2026年 11月 6日

1/7 
2014年 GPIF改革から考える国内債券の位置づけ 
～「金利のある世界」で現行配分は最適か～ 
 資産運用調査部 主席研究員  奥田 宏二

（要旨） 
○ GPIFが基本ポートフォリオを変更し、国内債券の割合を引き上げるのではないかとの観測が
市場で浮き沈みしている。本稿では、現在の基本ポートフォリオの起点となった 2014年の運
用改革の成立過程を分析し、変更する場合にどのようなことが起き得るのか考察する。 
○ 2014年改革では、基本ポートフォリオに占める国内債券を60％から 35％へ大幅に引き下げ、
国内外の株式を約 2倍に増やした。アベノミクスの象徴の一つとして GPIF改革は語られる
ことが多いが、改革につながる議論は民主党政権期から始まっていた。 
○ 当時のGPIF運用委員会の議事録によれば、株式をどこまで増やすかより、国内債券をどこま
で減らすかが議論の中心だった。また、当時決定した国内債券 35％という配分は、期待収益
率やリスクだけで機械的に決まったものではなく、移行コストや市場への影響などを考慮し
て決定された。市場への影響を抑えるため、正式決定前から乖離許容幅を使って国内債券を
減らしていたのは、今後の動向を見極めるうえで興味深い。当面、四半期の運用実績に注目
が集まるだろう。 
○ 足元では長期金利が上昇し、低金利が懸念点だった 2014年当時とは逆の環境にある。当時の
運用委員会では、金利が上がれば国内債券の期待収益率が高まり、最適化をやり直すことで
国債の割合が上昇し得るとの説明がなされていた。当時の考え方を踏まえれば、現在の基本
ポートフォリオが「金利のある世界」でも最適なのか再考の余地があろう。

### Page 2

2/7 
1. はじめに 
年金積立金管理運用独立行政法人（GPIF）を巡り、基本ポートフォリオの見直し観測が市場で
浮き沈みしている。きっかけは片山さつき財務・金融担当相が2026年7月、GPIFをはじめとする
年金基金について、日本の金融資産への投資をさらに増やす方向を後押ししたいとの考えを示し
たことだ。長期金利が上昇するなかでの発言で、日本の金融資産とは特に国内債券だと解されて
いる。こうしたなか、GPIF側では同年 8月の経営委員会の議事次第に「基本ポートフォリオ検証
等PTにおける議論について」が報告事項に入り、経営委員会への関心が高まった。 
GPIFの基本ポートフォリオは 5年に 1度、公的年金の財政検証を踏まえて見直す。現在の基本
ポートフォリオは2025年度が開始年だ。制度上、毎年度検証し、必要があれば 5年間の中期目標
期間中でも見直しを検討することになっているものの、GPIF発足後、中期目標期間の途中で基本
ポートフォリオを変更したのは 2013年 6月と 2014年 10月の 2回にとどまる。 
2014年10月の見直しでは、国内債券比率を 60％から 35％へ引き下げ、国内外の株式をそれぞ
れ12％から25％へ引き上げる大幅な変更が行われた。株式比率の上昇によって損失が大きくなる
リスクを懸念する向きはあったが、運用損失だけでなく、年金財政上、目標とする利回りを達成
できないこともリスクと捉える考え方を明確にした。2014年改革の本質は公的年金運用における
「安全」という概念を変えたことだと筆者はみている。 
では、仮にいま国内債券を再び増やすとなれば、改革の逆行を意味するのか。この問いを考え
るため、年金財政からの議論と成長戦略からの要請という二つの流れを民主党政権期からたど
り、2014年改革がどのように実現したのかを振り返る。

2. 民主党政権期――国債中心の「安全運用」への疑問 
2014年改革は第2次安倍晋三政権の経済政策「アベノミクス」の象徴の一つとして語られるこ
とが多い。しかし、その成立過程をさかのぼると、改革につながる議論は第 2次安倍政権の発足
前からあった。民主党政権期の議論では、公的年金の運用における「安全」を巡る議論が次第に
成熟していったことが確認できる。 
2006年に発足したGPIFの基本ポートフォリオは、当初国内債券が 67％を占めていた（資料 1）。
このうち、大半を占める国債は満期に額面で償還される。国債偏重の狙いは、厚生労働省の検討
会が 2010年12月にまとめた報告書にも表れている。公的年金運用の考え方として、保険料は
「投資を目的として徴収されたもの」ではないとして、「安全運用が基本」とした（注 1）。検討会
では、全額を国債で運用すべきだとの意見もあった。

### Page 3

3/7 
資料 1 GPIFの基本ポートフォリオの変遷

（注）2014年 10月以降は短期資産を基本ポートフォリオに含めない。 
（出所）GPIFの公表資料より第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

もっとも、国債を多く持つことが本当に安全なのかという疑問はすでにこの検討会で示されて
いた。運用上のリスクを単なる損失だけで捉えず、年金財政上予定された収益を下回り、将来の
保険料率の引き上げや給付の引き下げにつながる可能性まで考慮する必要があるとしている。国
債を多く持てば安全だという単純な整理には収まらない議論が存在していたことが確認できる。 
この検討会に続く社会保障審議会年金部会の専門委員会では、国債への集中はリスクになると
いう考えが強く示されるようになる。2012年 2月に同委員会の議論を整理した資料（注2）では
国債のみの運用は一つの資産への集中であり、分散投資ができていないという意味でリスクが非
常に大きい、という指摘が確認できる。デフレと低金利がこの先も続くとは考えにくく、物価や
賃金が上昇すれば、給付する年金額は増える一方で債券価格は下落し、損失を被る可能性が高い
というアベノミクス下での改革を先取りするような意見も出た。国債のみの運用で、将来にわた
り物価や賃金の上昇率を上回る利回りを確保できるのか疑問が残る、との意見もあった。 
民主党政権後期の議論は、短期の運用損失を過度に重視するのではなく、国債偏重によって年
金財政上、必要な利回りを確保できないリスクを考慮する方向へと発展していった。

3. 産業競争力会議――成長戦略からの見直し要請 
GPIF改革に至る過程では、日本経済の成長戦略の文脈で国債偏重の見直しを求める意見があっ
た。これは安倍政権時だけでなく民主党政権期にもあった。国内債券を減らすべきだという方向
は、年金財政からの議論と重なる。ただ、見直しを求める理由は両政権期とも公的年金の巨額の
資金を経済成長に生かすという発想から来ている。 
安倍政権でGPIF改革の舞台となったのは、政権発足直後の2013年 1月に始まった産業競争力会
議だ。「公的・準公的資金の有効活用」が初回から議論にのぼり、安全資産に偏った年金などの運
用を見直し、成長分野への資金供給につなげる考え方が示された。同会議が進むなかでは、民間

### Page 4

4/7 
議員から「アクティブ運用をし、そこに中長期でもの言う株主ということで企業が新陳代謝も進
めるようなツールとしていく」といった発言が飛び出している（注 3）。 GPIFの資金を企業改革や
経済成長に生かそうとする考え方であり、「専ら被保険者の利益」のために運用するという年金積
立金の原則との緊張をはらんでいた。

4. 有識者会議――成長戦略と被保険者利益の両立 
成長戦略のなかで出たこうした要請を、公的年金の運用原則とどのように両立させたのだろう
か。この問題に対応したのが「公的・準公的資金の運用・リスク管理等の高度化等に関する有識
者会議」だった。この会議は政府の日本再興戦略を受けて経済再生担当相の下に設置され、GPIF
改革の理論的柱を打ち立てた。 
公的年金の運用に国民が何を最も期待しているのか明確に定義した点がこの会議のポイントだ
ろう。2013年 11月の報告書は、「現役世代の保険料負担を維持しつつ、将来の年金給付に支障が
生じないよう、年金財政の長期的な健全性の確保に資すること」こそが国民の最も期待している
ことだとした（注4）。目標とする運用利回りが達成できなければ、保険料の引き上げか将来の年
金給付水準の引き下げが迫られる可能性が生じる。 
この定義に則り、デフレから脱却して適度なインフレ環境へ移るというアベノミクスの目指し
た環境でのポートフォリオのあり方を検討した。安全資産とされる国債を多く持てば安全だと考
えるのではなく、年金財政に必要な収益を確保できるかという観点から基本ポートフォリオを考
える方向が明確に示された。 
収益率の向上という文脈のなかで、産業競争力会議で出たようなGPIFの資金を企業改革に生か
すというアイデアは運用改革に取り込まれていく。投資先企業との対話や議決権行使を通じて企
業価値の向上を促し、その成果を運用収益として享受するという考え方が報告書で示された。成
長戦略が期待した企業改革を、被保険者の利益につながる運用につなげたのだ。 
社会保障審議会年金部会の専門委員会もこれに呼応する形で、デフレから脱却して適度なイン
フレ環境へ移る状況では、あらかじめ「国内債券中心」と示す必要はなく、GPIFが運用目的・目
標に沿ったポートフォリオを検討すべきだとした。年金積立金の運用目標は、年金給付費が長期
的には名目賃金上昇率に連動することから、賃金上昇率をどの程度上回るかで設定される。同専
門委員会は、財政検証に向けて検討した複数の経済前提を踏まえ、GPIFが確保すべき運用利回り
として、名目賃金上昇率を 1.7％上回る水準を示した（注5）。

5. GPIF運用委員会――国内債券 35％の決定過程 
ここから2014年 10月の基本ポートフォリオ変更に至る議論を、当時のGPIF運用委員会の議事
録からたどる。2014年改革は、株式を増やした改革とみられがちだ。しかし、運用委員会の議論
の中心になったのは株式の比率ではなく、国内債券の扱いだった。後年公表された議事録を読む
と、国内債券中心では目標の利回りが達成できないどころか、金利が上昇した場合には多額の損
失を被るといった懸念が、当時の事務局や理事長から示されている。当時の長期金利は 0.5％前後

### Page 5

5/7 
と、歴史的な低水準にあった（資料 2）。事務局の試算も、国内債券の比率を 45％以下に減らさな
ければ目標の利回りに届かない、という形で示された。

資料 2 日本国債 10年物利回り

（注）10年物国債利回りの月末値。2026年 11月は 6日の値。 
（出所）Bloombergより第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

GPIF内部の議論では株式をどこまで増やすかではなく、国内債券をどこまで減らすかが議論の
焦点だったことが分かる。運用委員会の委員長は最終段階で、「国債を減らしたというのが一番の
主で、その結果として、こういうポートフォリオが書けた」と振り返っているのが象徴的だろう
（注 6）。 
基本ポートフォリオの決定に至る議論で興味深いのは、GPIFの売買が国内市場に与える影響を
相当程度考慮に入れていた点だ。事務局から示された基本ポートフォリオ候補は当初、五つあっ
た。このなかには、現在のポートフォリオである 4資産を 25％ずつとする案も含まれていた。こ
の案は、現状からの移行を考えると「現実感がゼロ」とされ、早い段階で検証の対象から外れた
（注 7）。事務局は候補を絞り込む際に足元の資産構成からの距離を物差しに加え、最終案の選定
理由にも「足下の資産構成と相対的に近く、移行に係るコストが小さい」ことを挙げている（注
8）。最終段階では、1％刻みで確認すると、経済中位ケースでは国内債券 34％などのより効率的な
配分も見つかった。それでも、国内債券をさらに減らして国内株式を増やせば移行の影響が大き
くなるとして、35％が維持された。外国市場に比べ、国内市場ではGPIFの売買の影響が大きいと
いう判断が働いていた。 
国内債券の売却は、正式な決定を待たずに始まっていた。運用委員会は2014年4月の時点で、
現行の基本ポートフォリオが認める範囲の中で国内債券を減らす方針を了承している。委員長は

### Page 6

6/7 
その目的を、金利上昇に備えて「債券の評価損を抑える」ことだと確認した（注9）。8月には、
その範囲を超えて国内債券を売り、ほかの資産に振り向ける方針も了承された。事務局は、積立
金規模が巨額であるため、市場インパクトやフロントランニングを誘発せずに速やかに資産配分
を変更することは現実的ではないと説明し、基本ポートフォリオの正式決定前から資産配分の変
更を検討する考えを示した（注 10）。正式決定に先立つ 8月末には、国内債券の比率はすでに
50％程度まで下がっていた。 
GPIFの現在の中期目標でも、市場の価格形成や民間の投資行動等を歪めないことが求められて
いる。今後、基本ポートフォリオを変更する場合にも、市場への影響を抑えるため、正式な変更
に先立って資産配分を調整することが考えられる。今回も運用の動向を見極めるうえで、四半期
の運用実績が注目される。

6. おわりに 
2014年にGPIFが中期目標期間の途中で基本ポートフォリオを変更した背景には、先行きの運用
環境の変化を見通した判断があった。GPIFは当時、日本経済が「長年続いたデフレからの転換と
いう大きな運用環境の変化の節目」にあるとし、「長期的な経済環境の変化に速やかに対応する観
点から、来年度を待たず」に変更に踏み切った（注 11）。実際には低金利がその後も長く続いた
が、足元では当時懸念されていた金利上昇が現実のものとなっている。 
長期金利は2026年 9月に 3％台に達し、約 30年ぶりの水準を付けた。奇しくも3％という数字
は、2014年のGPIF運用委員会でもたびたび登場していた。当時の理事長は、国内債券 60％という
従来の基本ポートフォリオについて、「国内債券の利回りが 3％というものを大前提としてつくっ
ている」と述べていた（注 12）。  
興味深いのは、2014年の運用委員会で、金利が上昇すれば国内債券の将来の期待収益率が高ま
り、改めて資産配分の最適化を行うことで「自然に国債の割合がまたもとに戻るというか、上が
っていく」とGPIFの事務局側が説明していたことだ（注 13）。 2020年の基本ポートフォリオ変更
では、国内金利の低下を踏まえ、国内債券を減らして外国債券を増やした。今後は、長期の収益
確保を担う株式の比率を維持しつつ、為替リスクなどを考慮して外国債券の一部を国内債券に振
り向けることも選択肢として考えられる。2014年に国内債券を減らしたのと同じ考え方が、今度
はその割合を引き上げる判断につながり得るのではないだろうか。

Wiwynn
Valuation
Our target price for Wiwynn is NT$3,200. We value Wiwynn based on PE as we believe the company’s earnings performance
and industry dynamics will be the key focus areas for the market. Our TP is based on 16x 2027E EPS, which is at the top end of
the historical trading range since listing and above the average PE multiple of the peer group. We believe our target multiple is
justified by the company’s 100% exposure to data centers, rising scale, and robust earnings growth, as well as stabilizing ROE.

Risks
Key downside risks that could cause Wiwynn’s shares to trade below our target price include: 1) Potential volatility in capex
deployment; 2) Component price hike; 3) Peer competition; and 4) FX volatility.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: We review GCE’s 3Q26 results, released on 10 November.

What’s the impact: 3Q26 beat Bloomberg consensus. GCE’s 3Q26 
earnings came in at TWD6.39bn (EPS: TWD12.4), in line with our estimate 
and 3% higher vs. the street’s. While revenue was 7% lower than our 
expectation due to flooding in Thailand and product transition, gross margin 
expanded by 4pp QoQ to 38.7% mainly due to easing raw-material cost 
inflation and its own price increases. However, net income was impacted by 
higher non-op loss of TWD238m (likely due to FX loss) though offset by a 
lower effective tax rate of 25% as Thailand plant turned more profitable.

Business outlook. Looking ahead, GCE now guides for revenue to be 
flattish QoQ in 4Q26. We suspect that AMZN’s ASIC AI server gradually 
shifting its model from air-cooled (2 chipsets per mainboard) to liquid-
cooled (4 chipsets) and the year-end inventory-taking are the key reasons. 
However, we expect the stagnant revenue momentum to be short-lived and 
growth momentum to resume with new projects (including Google’s TPU 
and MSFT’s Maia) contributing from 1H27.

In 2H27, we expect new capacity ramps at its 2nd plant in Thailand and 2nd 
plant in Suzhou (acquisition of Pegatron’s land; across the 1st plant in 
Suzhou) to be growth drivers, which could further increase its allocation in 
major ASIC AI server projects. As for concerns over the thicker-HDI board 
(a combination of sequential lamination HLC and HDI together) that we 
highlighted in in our previous note (2Q26 results review: profitability missed 
slightly), we believe GCE has already overcome the difficulties for 3-
lamination HDI and can now successfully produce AMZN’s requirement on 
its Trn4 AI server heavy HDI board. However, GCE might still need to 
achieve the requirement for Google’s TPU v9 which requires c.5-6 
lamination techniques. We believe the capacity expansion in 2027 would 
not be enough to catch up with AI demand growth in 2027. As such, we 
believe it will further drive price hikes in CCL raw-material, CCL, and PCB, 
and see gross margin (GM) expansion over our forecast horizon. We raise 
our GM forecasts to 40.5%/41.3% (prior: 38.0/40.2%) for 2027-28E.

What we recommend: We raise our 2027-28E EPS by 1-10% to reflect its 
3Q26 results and higher GM on current supply-demand dynamics. We 
reaffirm our Buy (1) call but trim our 12M TP to TWD1,600 with a lower 
PER target of 21x (prior: 26x; vs. past-3-year range of 7-28x), on our 4-
quarter-forward diluted EPS, to reflect weaker market sentiment and on-
going concerns over HDI. Key risk: worse-than-expected AI server demand.

How we differ: Our 2027-28E earnings are 4-5% higher than consensus, 
likely due to our higher profitability assumptions.

10 November 2026

3Q26 review: results largely in line with our estimates

➢ GM expands with spec upgrade and favourable pricing environment 
➢ New client and new products likely to be key growth drivers in 2027 
➢ Maintaining Buy (1) rating; trimming 12M TP to TWD1,600

Company profile 
Founded in 1981 and listed on the Taiwan Stock Exchange in 1998, Gold Circuit Electronics (GCE) was, 
in its early years, one of the world’s top-3 printed circuit board (PCB) vendors providing double-sided 
and multi-layer PCBs for notebooks. However, as the notebook market became saturated from 2011, 
GCE started reshuffling its revenue mix to more high-end products (servers, workstations and 
networking boards). The requirement of higher-layer PCBs from these products has not only proved 
GCE’s manufacturing capabilities but also created a high entry barrier. GCE’s revenue contribution from 
the server and networking segments has surged to over 80% in 2024 (from 37% in 2010), the highest 
among its PCB peers.

### Page 5

5

Gold Circuit Electronics (2368 TT): 10 November 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 10 Nov 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 2
GCE's Board consists of 9 directors, including 3 independent directors. The number of
independent directors is above Taiwan securities law's requirement of more than two and
exceeds one-fifth of the total number of directors on the board. The backgrounds of the
independent directors are diverse with expertise in the areas of business management, finance
& audit, and law, which should be positive for executive and board quality.
That said, the roles of CEO and chairman are held by one person, who is a second-generation
family member of GCE’s founder. The organisation structure appears to us to be a family
business, which could lead to conflicts of interest between key executives and outside
shareholders.
Capital management 2
GCE declared a DPS of TWD10.0 in 2025, another record high. GCE has declared cash
dividends for six consecutive years from 2020 to 2025, following a period of intermittent payouts
mainly due to fierce competition in the PCB industry (especially in notebook applications) and
high earnings volatility from its 3 plants in China caused by historically low utilisation rates. We
believe the company’s strategy to focus on high-end products (eg, servers and networking) has
started bearing fruit, which was also the main reason GCE was able to declare a record-high
cash dividend in 2025. Overall, we expect GCE to continue to benefit from ASP and gross-
margin expansion, driven by server and networking spec upgrades, which should be value-
accretive for investors in the long term.
Related party &
transaction 1 Sales to related parties were 0% of total 2025 sales and purchases from related parties were also 0% of total 2025 purchases. We see limited risk from related-party transactions.
S Supply chain management 1
GCE is a multi-layer PCB manufacturer with major raw materials, such as thin core, copper foil,
pre-preg, copper ball, dry film, solder mask and various chemical materials used in the
manufacturing process. GCE has acquired the ISO 9001 and IECQ QC 080000 certifications to
improve its supply chain management capabilities in product quality and hazardous material
usage. Like most hardware vendors, GCE is also committed to refusing procurement of conflict
minerals and complying with the Responsible Business Alliance (RBA) Code of Conduct.
According to its 2025 annual report, over 99% of its top 40 suppliers returned their RBA
commitment letters. As part of its RBA compliance efforts, GCE holds an annual supply chain
meeting to review the achievement rate of its previous targets and set new goals for suppliers. In
2025, GCE audited 154 suppliers, including 114 on-site audits and 40 document reviews,
achieving a 100% completion rate against its audit targets. The company also evaluates new
suppliers based on ISO 9001, ISO 14001 and IATF 16949 certification requirements and
conducts annual supplier assessments.
S Data security 2
Information security is one of the key topics under the company’s risk management guidelines.
GCE constructed an information security system, under the “information security committee”, to
minimise the risk of information loss, sabotage, and leakage caused by human errors, malicious
destructions or natural disasters. Below are several measures the company has taken to
improve data security: GCE will conduct regular awareness training to ensure employees'
security awareness and fulfilment of information security system requirements. Also, the
information security committee will develop an information security continuity plan and conduct
drills to ensure continuous operation of the company.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

Gold Circuit Electronics (2368 TT): 10 November 2026

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: Following on our results Memo, we lower our forecasts and 
TP for M31, as we factor in its still suboptimal scale and delayed 
employment bonus in 3Q26 that weigh on its trajectory of a bottom-line 
turnaround. That said, we remain comfortable with our Buy (1) call on the 
stock as we expect M31’s turnaround, kicking off from 3Q26 despite at a 
small scale, to sustain into 2027 with meaningful incremental builds, 
supported by its conversion from license to royalty revenue paying off, and 
broadened footprint into advanced technologies for price accretion and into 
diversified foundry, IDM, and fabless customers for volume accretion.

What’s the impact: Forecast changes. We lower our 2026-27E EPS for 
M31 by 14-43% to embed its still suboptimal scale and opex overhang in 
our model. Despite the cuts, its bottom-line turnaround met our expectation, 
and we expect the turnaround to sustain with meaningful incremental builds 
sequentially through 2027 to improve market confidence. We forecast its 
4Q26 top line to rebound by 23% QoQ (+12% YoY) for EPS to reach 
TWD3.31 (TWD0.35 in 3Q26), setting a good start for M31’s 2027 outlook 
and warranting a strong 213% EPS CAGR over 2026-28E.

Turnaround confirmed. While prolonged license project recognitions 
delayed M31’s turnaround, depicted in our previous Flash, we believe its 
bumpy operations over the past 2 years is finally coming to an end, 
evidenced by management’s comments of QoQ revenue growth for 4Q26 
with EPS expanding to cover accumulated losses YTD. M31 has actively 
invested over the past years enriching its IP portfolio, accumulating more 
than 2,000 license projects since 2012 (chart on p.2), and setting a good 
foundation for converting them to royalty revenue. It aims for the royalty 
revenue to outgrow license revenue in the next few quarters, after years of 
“cooking”. Meanwhile, two structural growth drivers remain intact for ASP 
and volume accretion: 1) for ASP, a growing number of high-value projects 
from advanced process nodes to enhance M31’s IP portfolio for ASP 
accretion, and 2) for volume, an increased diversification of customer base 
globally to broaden its addressable markets for volume accretion.

What we recommend: Given our forecast revisions, we lower our 12M TP 
for M31 to TWD550 (previous: TWD645), still based on our 4-quarter 
forward PER of 45x. We reaffirm our Buy (1) rating with key downside risks 
of silicon inflation and geopolitical overhang leading to global inflation and 
AI capex cut to delay M31’s royalty ramps.

How we differ: Our 2027E EPS is 52% above the consensus, likely due to 
our more positive view on royalty revenue ramp.

10 November 2026

Structural turnaround round the corner (III)

➢ Lowering our forecasts to reflect still suboptimal scale… 
➢ … but expecting enriched license projects to bear fruit soon 
➢ Reiterating our Buy (1) rating with a lower 12M TP of TWD550

Company profile 
Founded in 2011, M31 is an independent semiconductor IP provider in Taiwan, offering both foundation 
IP for specialty technology nodes and functional IP such as high-speed interface and analogue IP.

M31 Technology (6643 TT): 10 November 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 11 Mar 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 2
M31's board of directors (BoD) has 7 members, including 3 independent directors, who
represent 43% of total directors, higher than the regulatory requirement of at least 20%. Its
Chairman and President are not the same person, but its President is one of the directors. M31
has the Audit Committee, Compensation Committee and Risk Control Committee under the
BoD.
Capital management 1
Although M31 is a young company (founded in 2011 and listed in 2019), its dividend payout ratio
has been over the 75% level for the past 5 years. M31 is a semiconductor IP provider without
onsite production activities, which would require high capex to do so. As such, we view its high
dividend payout ratio to be appropriate.
Related party &
transaction 1 We do not see any meaningful impact from related-party transactions.
S Supply chain management 2
M31's suppliers are required to comply with the Supplier Management Standards, which was set
by M31 in 2013. In addition, M31 has periodical evaluations for its suppliers and onsite audits to
ensure the quality and implementation of Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR), as well as
Environmental Protection and Safety and Integrity Policies.
E Water & wastewater management 1 M31 is a semiconductor IP provider without physical products, and thus no production activity to affect the environment.
E
Waste & hazardous
materials
management
1
The company is a semiconductor IP provider without physical products, and thus no production
activity to affect the environment. It still adopts some measures to reduce waste from its
operations, such as recycling and using electronic files to replace paper documents as much as
possible to reduce the use of paper.
E GHG emissions 1 M31 is a semiconductor IP provider without physical products, and thus no production activity to affect the environment.

Key Data  _____________________________________

GS Forecast  __________________________________

GS Factor Profile  ______________________________

AWSC’s 3Q26 core business OP was NT$209mn, which was lower 
than both GSe/BBG consensus at NT$226mn/NT$250mn, mainly 
due to the ongoing increase in raw material cost, leading to a 24.3% 
GM (lower than GSe/BBG consensus GM at 25.6%/27.8%), despite 
UTR in 3Q26 remaining stable at 65-70%. Moreover, AWSC reported 
NI of NT$191mn, which was 3% higher than GSe but 17% lower than 
the BBG consensus due to the GM contraction. 
Going into 4Q26, we believe the company’s revenue will increase by 
1% as key business lines (including WiFi, cellular and VCSEL) should 
remain stable in 4Q26 as we expect the company’s utilization rate to 
remain stable at 65-70%. Moreover, we expect its GM to rise to 
27.2% (up 2.9ppt QoQ) due to the material cost increase slowing 
down. 
We continue to view rising material costs as the key risk to AWSC’s 
profitability, as the company is now securing raw materials at higher 
cost as low-cost inventory has already been depleted in 1H26 amid 
the strong demand pull in. The company continues to negotiate 
price increases with clients and expects the adjustments to be fully 
reflected starting in 1Q27. However, we remain cautious on the 
low-end cellular segment, where customer demand tends to be 
more price-sensitive. 
For the AI-related business, the company continues to ship VCSEL 
products for 400G/800G optical transceiver this year, with revenue 
contribution to be <2% in 2026E per our estimates. Meanwhile, the 
company continues to make progress on the new PD (Photodiode) 
product and is currently undergoing verification with several 
customers. The company expects PD products to start seeing 
revenue contribution starting from 2H27 or 2027-end given its long 
verification process, typically 6 months+, thus we expect revenue 
contribution to be limited at <2%/~5% in 2027E/2028E. 
Investment view

### Page 3

We maintain our conservative view on the cellular/WiFi PA industry (see here) as we 
continue to see weaker smartphone shipment (-10%/+2%/+2% in 2026/27/28, see 
here) as the smartphone market continues to be impacted by the elevated memory price 
(see here). 
For AWSC, we believe the company could further gain market share among China PA 
design houses as Chinese clients increasingly shift toward higher-end PA products, 
supported by the company’s superior cost control capability and higher production yield 
relative to peers. In addition, AWSC has continued to expand its market share with a 
Korean smartphone brand, which should provide further growth momentum to its 
cellular business in coming quarters/years. Overall, we expect AWSC to continue gaining 
share from its key competitors due to (1) its competitive pricing strategy, underpinned 
by a more favorable cost structure comparing to its key competitor, Win Semi 
(AWSC/Win Semi’s fixed cost accounts for 30%/40% of the total, while Win Semi’s COGS 
per wafer is ~130% higher than AWSC’s) and (2) its strategic focus on China- and 
US-based PA design houses. 
We continue to see a recovery in 2026 CAPEX plans among Taiwan PA foundry players. 
However, we do not view this as a signal of a meaningfully stronger demand outlook as 
the capital spending is more related to new AI businesses. AWSC revised up its 2026 
CAPEX guidance to NT$600-650mn (from NT$500-600mn) and continues to expect 
CAPEX to increase in 2027/28. Also, management continues to expect UTR to reach at 
most ~70% in 2026, implying no need for capacity expansion in the near term while the 
additional capex will be spent on new optical related business. 
Overall, we maintain our Neutral rating on AWSC with a new 12-m TP of NT$136 (from 
NT$147) as we roll over our valuation period by one quarter. Post results, we revise 
down our 2027E/28E earnings by 10%/10% to reflect lower revenue and the negative 
impact from rising material cost (GM contraction), revise up 2026E earnings by 2%, and 
introduce our 2029E estimates.

### Page 5

Earnings revisions  
We revise up our 2026E revenue by 1% to factor in the 3Q26 actual results and we revise 
down 2027E/28E revenue by 9%/10% assuming a more conservative outlook for the 
cellular business; we revise down 2026E/27E/28E GM estimates by 0.4/0.7/0.8ppt to 
reflect the negative impact from rising material cost. Overall, we revise down 2027E/28E 
earnings by 10%/10% and revise up 2026E earnings by 2%. 
In this report, we introduce our 2029E estimates.

Valuation 
We maintain our Neutral rating and revise down our TP from NT$147 to NT$136, based 
on a lower 25x target P/E (from 27x previously, 0.5 STDV below the company’s past 
10-year forward P/E multiple), factoring in our earnings revisions and, consistent with 
our group coverage practices, rolling over our valuation period from 4Q26-3Q27E to 
2027E.

### Page 6

Investment Thesis and TP & risks

AWSC is one of the largest GaAS foundries globally, with 10-15% market share in the 
GaAS foundry industry in 2025. We expect the company to enjoy solid share gain 
opportunity in the long term, considering its much lower than peers’ production cost 
and better production efficiency. However, given the high inventory level in the supply 
chain, and the slower-than-expected Android smartphone PA demand in coming 
quarters, we expect relatively slow profit growth in the near term. 
We are Neutral rated given the company’s relatively slow profit growth in coming 
quarters. We think the stock is priced fairly, trading at its historical upcycle average P/E 
multiple, despite the long-term market share growth opportunity. We forecast AWSC’s 
net income to grow at a 34% 2026E-28E CAGR. The key up/downside risks are (1) 
easing/rising competition in the China PA foundry market, (2) 
faster/slower-than-expected progress on 5G/WiFi 7 upgrade trends, and (3) 
faster/slower-than expected progress with new businesses, including LEO satellite, 
drones and datacom.  
Valuation methodology: Our 12-month target price of NT$136 is based on a target 25x 
2027E P/E, which is 0.5 STDV below the company’s past 10-year forward P/E multiple 
and the inventory build-up period average P/E. We consider this multiple appropriate 
given the soft demand outlook and our positive view on the company’s longer-term 
share gain opportunity. 
The key up/downside risks are 1) easing/rising competition in the China PA foundry 
market, (2) faster/slower-than-expected progress on 5G/WiFi 7 upgrade trends, and (3) 
faster/slower-than expected progress with new businesses, including LEO satellite, 
drones and datacom.

Ratings, coverage universe and related definitions
Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.
Coverage Universe: A list of all stocks in each coverage universe is available by primary analyst, stock and coverage universe at

https://www.gs.com/research/hedge.html.

大量(3167 TT) Buy

CPO 設備將於 3Q27 量產，給 Buy 的投資建議。 
投資建議：大量受惠於 AI 帶動 PCB 製造、半導體檢測需求，
獲利大幅成長，CPO 設備將於 3Q27 量產，給 Buy 的投資建議。 
大量受惠於 AI 伺服器帶動高階 PCB 鑽孔設備：大量在±2 mil 
PCB 背鑽計量領域的地位依然穩固；大量受惠於 AI 伺服器帶
動高階 PCB 鑽孔設備需求的趨勢，而非單純受惠某一客戶的拉
貨。 
3Q26 毛利率季增約 6pts： 大量 3Q26 營收 36.71 億元，
QoQ+28.40%；稅後純益 8.99 億元，QoQ+65.51%；稅後 EPS 9.85
元。 
3Q26 毛利率季增約 6pts，主要來自產品組合優化，包含高階
CCD 機種及 TM3、 TM4。一般機種毛利率約 20~30%，高階機
種毛利率約 50%， TM3、 TM4 毛利率更高，TM4 於 3Q26 出貨
約 5 台。 
大量的子公司-大量先進智能規劃 IPO：大量先進智能已經於
08/01/2026 獨立，目前大量持股大量先進智能 100%，預計大量
先進智能上櫃後，大量持股大量先進智能仍會超過 60%。

(Metrology)、 AOI 智慧檢測(Inspection)及 (Automation)自動化。
產品應用包含 CoWoS、 SoIC 及面板級封裝(FOPLP)(CoPoS)、
CPO 等。

2 群益投顧 
  ESG：  
E：(Environmental)。大量科技秉持「愛護地球、永續發展」之理念，持續推
動環境管理制度，並透過日常營運管理落實環境保護與污染預防，降低營運
活動對環境造成之影響。大量科技秉持「愛護地球、永續發展」之理念，持
續推動環境管理制度，並透過日常營運管理落實環境保護與污染預防，降低
營運活動對環境造成之影響。大量科技針對廠區內原物料、製程、生產活動
及服務建立環境考量評估機制，評估範圍涵蓋正常、異常及緊急狀況下可能
產生之環境影響，並納入空氣排放、水污染、廢棄物、能源使用、土地污染、
噪音及其他環境衝擊等項目。 
S：(Social)。大量科技重視人才發展與員工照顧，將員工視為公司穩健營運
與永續經營的重要基礎。大量科技秉持以人為本的精神，致力於培育與留任
專業人才，並透過多元招募、薪酬福利、教育訓練、勞資溝通及權益保障等
制度，持續打造友善、平等且具支持性的職場環境。大量科技於人才任用上
遵循相關勞動法令及多元平等原則，重視員工專業能力發展及工作權益保
障，並持續透過教育訓練提升員工技能，強化員工與公司共同成長之基礎。
G：(Governance)。大量科技致力於推動產品朝高規格、高自動化、高智能化
及完全整合化之方向發展，以貼近客戶需求，並與客戶共同開發新產品。此
外，公司持續投入自動化設備之研發，厚植核心技術能力，並持續開發進階
產品，以掌握市場先機。為確保制度有效運作，大量科技於企劃室下設企業
誠信經營小組，由總經理擔任召集人，負責誠信經營政策之推動與執行情形
之監督，並每年向董事會報告執行成果及防範不誠信行為之措施。大量科技
依董事會通過之「誠信經營守則」及相關內部規章，建立對於不誠信行為之
相關防範機制，並透過內部控制、檢舉制度及教育訓練等措施，確保誠信經
營政策之落實。 
公司簡介： 
大量的產主要產品應用於製造、半導體先進製程與先進封裝，以及光電、玻
璃基板與精密材料加工等領域。透過自主研發之控制器、應用軟體、視覺定
位、深度控制、運動控制及量測檢測技術，大量科技依客戶製程需求提供設
備及技術服務。 
PCB 設備：PCB 鑽孔定位、PCB 成型切割、邊緣滾塗、針塗等塗佈製程及
高速型內層銅深度量測機等。 
半導體設備：CMP Pad 即時量測監控設備、3D IC 先進封裝製程晶圓及晶片
量測設備、FOPLP 形貌量測設備、3DIC 先進封裝製程檢測設備、Flip Chip 
製程檢測設備、捲帶包裝晶片本體及外觀缺陷之檢查設備等、封裝製程自動
化設備等。 
大量 PCB 客戶的客戶包含台灣、中國的 PCB 廠；半導體設備客戶包含晶圓
代工廠、OSAT。  
高階背鑽設備的市場，主要同業為德國 Schmoll Maschinen。  
資料來源：大量年報。

2027 年 PCB 與半導體設備展望皆正向： 
PCB 設備 2027 年每月都有要交機，交期約 5~6 月；半導體設備 B/B ratio 維
持在 2 以上。 
大量預估 2027 年 CCD 成型機與鑽孔機將維持雙位數成長，TM3 完成階段
性任務後出貨將減少，TM4 出貨量預期會有大幅倍數成長。PCB 在手訂單
有達千台。

5 群益投顧 
大量有 CPO 相關設備，單價較一般半導體設備高： 
大量目前與北美 Tier 1 客戶合作，2026 年預計完成第一條產線的建置，量
產時程預計落在 3Q27。 2027 年設備需求量將倍數成長。 
由於該設備為整合光學前處理、檢測、後處理的一體機，結合了 3~4 項關鍵
技術，平均售價(ASP)約為一般晶圓檢測設備的 300~400%。  
Forward PE Band

8 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】 
  評等 定義 
  強力買進(Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間≥ 35%  
  買進(Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間<35%  
  區間操作(Trading Buy) 5%≤首次評等潛在上漲空間<15%

中立(Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等 
預期近期股價將處於盤整 
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】 
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。  
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。 
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」、「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。 
停止推薦情境： 
1. 達目標價。 
2. 雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。 
3. 推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】 
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

(012450) 
한화에어로스페이스 
2026.10.30

AI 로보틱스/방산/조선/운송 양승윤 
6139 / syyang0901@eugenefn.com

여전히 비중확대 의견, 그리고 Top-Pick 
 투자의견 BUY(유지) 
 목표주가 1,660,000 원(유지) 
 현재주가 760,000 원(10/29)

 3Q26 연결 매출액은 6조 943억원(-6.0%yoy), 영업이익은 4,520억원(-
46.2%yoy, OPM 7.4%)을 기록. 영업이익 컨센 1조 1,614억원을 하회.  
 지상방산의 손익이 크게 악화. 영업이익은 217억원(OPM 1.7%) 기록. 주요 
납품이 4분기에 집중된 가운데 일회성 비용 약 500억원(대전사업장 복구·
정상화 약 300억원, 국내 사업 충당금 약 160억원)과 환율 영향이 겹침.   
 다만 내용을 살펴보면 수출 사업의 수익성 훼손은 크지 않았다는 판단임.  
 일회성 제외 영업이익은 약 717억원(OPM 5.8%)이며, 일회성 비용은 대전
사업장이 국내 물량을 전담한다는 점에서 전액 국내 귀속으로 추정. 내수 
OPM을 일회성 제외 -5% 내외로 가정하면, 내수 영업이익은 약 -826억원
(일회성 포함 OPM -12.7%), 수출은 1,043억원(OPM 17.6%)으로 추정됨. 
 1Q26 대비로는 국내 마진 하락이, 2Q26 대비로는 수출 물량 감소가 가장 
크게 작용(도표 참고). 국내는 저마진 사업 매출이 늘고 고마진 반복 양산 물
량이 줄며 일시적으로 믹스가 악화. 수출은 폴란드 등 주요 인도가 정체되어 
매출이 감소한 가운데 저수익 사업 중심으로 믹스가 악화된 영향. 
 4Q26부터 회복 구간 진입 전망. 폴란드 K9 2차 실행계약 물량의 현지 인
도가 본격화되며, 회사는 2026년 폴란드 K9 30문 이상, 천무 발사대 60대 
이상(*연초 40대에서 상향) 인도를 계획. 수출입 데이터상으로도 폴란드·루
마니아향 출하가 대규모로 진행 중이며 폴란드향 부속류 수출도 정상적으로 
이어지는 것으로 확인. 일회성 소멸과 고마진 수출 집중으로 수출 OPM은 
30% 내외로 회복할 것. 다만 국내는 정상 가동(2027년 상반기 목표) 전까
지 한 자릿수 초반 마진에 머물 것으로 전망. 
 중장기 수익성 우려도 크지 않다는 판단. 2027~2029년 매출 연 15% 성
장 가이던스는 유지됐고, 내년 폴란드 K9·천무 인도 물량은 모두 올해 대비 
증가할 계획. 폴란드 사업 이후 높아진 수출 마진 레벨은 후속 사업에서도 
유지될 전망. 수주는 중동 지정학 불안으로 지연되고 있으나 대공체계 등 문
의가 이어지고 있어, 2027년부터 중동이 수주의 중심이 될 것으로 기대. 
 동사에 대한 투자의견 BUY, 목표주가 166만원을 유지함. 3Q26 지상방산 
쇼크는 국내 적자와 수출 물량 공백이 겹친 일시적 결과로, 수출 경쟁력 훼
손으로 보기 어려움. 실적 충격에 따른 주가 조정은 분할 매수 기회로 판단.  
 한화에어로스페이스의 주가는 4월 7일 사상 최고가 153.7만원에서 10월 
29일 76.0만원으로 -51% 조정 받은 상황. 12MF P/E 기준 14.3x, 27E 
P/E는 13.9x로 밸류에이션 관점에서도 매력적인 구간 진입. 그동안 3분기 
실적 우려 등으로 부진했다면, 이제는 실적 바텀아웃과 대형 수주 랠리 속 
다시 한 번 반등을 기록할 차례. 동사를 방산업종 Top-pick으로 유지함.

Compliance Notice 
당사는 자료 작성일 기준으로 지난 3 개월 간 해당종목에 대해서 유가증권 발행에 참여한 적이 없습니다 
당사는 본 자료 발간일을 기준으로 해당종목의 주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다  
당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 어떠
한 경우에도 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

투자기간 및 투자등급/투자의견 비율 
종목추천 및 업종추천 투자기간: 12 개월  (추천기준일 종가대비 추천종목의 예상 목표수익률을 의미함) 당사 투자의견 비율(%) 
ㆍSTRONG BUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +50%이상 3% 
ㆍBUY(매수) 추천기준일 종가대비 +15%이상 ~ +50%미만 94% 
ㆍHOLD(중립) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%이상 ∼ +15%미만 3% 
ㆍREDUCE(매도) 추천기준일 종가대비 -10%미만 0% 
(2026.09.30 기준)

Document 318: Energy shock_ Dashboard 2026 vs. 2022 - November 2026 (2026-11-13)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Energy shock_ Dashboard 2026 vs. 2022 - November 2026 (2026-11-13).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 1
Status: ok

### Page 1

Energy shock: Dashboard 2026 vs. 2022
11/13/2026
2
Will
the
same
causes
produce
the
same
effects?
In
other
words,
will
the
war
in
Iran
and
the
resulting
surge
in
oil
and
gas
prices
lead
to
an
inflationary
shock
comparable
to
that
seen
in
2022
?
Will
their
negative
effects
on
growth
be
the
same
as
those
of
the
war
in
Ukraine
and
the
subsequent
energy
shock?
We
have
selected
a
set
of
indicators
to
track
the
impact
of
this
new
energy
shock
—
caused
by

the
war
in
the
Middle
East
—
on
activity
and
prices
in
the
Eurozone,
the
United
States,
oil
and
gas
markets
and
emerging
countries,
and
to
see
how
much
the
current
situation
resembles
that
of
2022
at
the
outbreak
of
the
conflict
in
Ukraine
.
This
update
marks
the
end
of
the
publication
of
this
graphical
and
commented
dashboard
.
The
cyclical
monitoring
continues
in
our
other
publications
.
Globally,
based
on
the
data
available
up
to
October
2026
,

the
2026
energy
shock
has
had
a
less
pronounced
impact
on
activity
and
inflation
than
that
of
2022
.
The
deterioration
in
business
climate
surveys
following
the
outbreak
of
the
conflict
in
Iran
was,
overall,
less
severe,
and
an
improvement
followed
fairly
quickly
,
driven
by
the
positive
impact
of
the
AI
boom
.
Household
confidence
has
been
more
affected
by
the
rise
in
energy
prices,
without
benefiting
from
the
cushioning
effect
of
the
AI
boom
.
On

the
emerging
markets
side,
while
non
-
resident
portfolio
investment
flows
have
been
highly
volatile
since
the
start
of
the
conflict
in
Iran,
financing
conditions
have
held
up
well
and
continue
to
withstand
both
the
energy
shock
and
the
pressures
on
advanced
economies'
sovereign
rates
.
The
rise
in
inflation
is,
for
its
part,
significant
but
remains,
for
the
time
being,
essentially
due
to
the
increase
in
energy
prices
.
Oil
and
gas
prices
have
experienced

significant
fluctuations
with

Document 320: Europe’s Deal Limiting Chinese Auto Imports (2026-11-02)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Europe’s Deal Limiting Chinese Auto Imports (2026-11-02).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 16
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
Europe’s Deal Limiting Chinese Auto Imports
Europe’s auto deal with China is a good start. But containing China’s export boom
will require a much broader response.
European Commissioner for Trade and Economic Security Maros Sefcovic speaks at a press briefing at the EU Delegation to
China in Beijing, China October 9, 2026. Florence Lo/Reuters
PUBLISHED
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
November 2, 2026 11:02 a.m.
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
The year-over-year increase in Chinese automobile
exports to all markets is currently about 3.5 million
cars.
Over ten years, that is 35 million cars. The global
market excluding China is only around 60 million
cars. Japan raised its auto exports by about 3.5 million
cars in the 1970s—but that was over a decade, not
one year. And right now the U.S. and Indian markets

(around 20 million cars) are effectively closed to
Chinese production. The 2026 jump in exports is an
incredible.
China’s domestic market, though, is down 20 percent
this year—that works out to a decline of about 5
million cars. The Wall Street Journal estimates that
domestic sales will fall to around 19 million cars this
year, less than a third of the production capacity of
China’s auto industry.
So even though China’s exports are increasing at an

unprecedented pace, China’s domestic auto sector
isn’t really booming; production is essentially flat, and
intense domestic competition is destroying the
profitability of firms that don’t export.*

### Page 3

3
Europe’s new auto deal with China should be
understood in this context.
The deal limits China’s exports of hybrids (including
plug-in hybrids) to Europe to around 350,000 cars.
That is above the 300,000 hybrids that China
exported to Europe in 2025, but well below the (gulp)
800,000 annualized pace for hybrids in the last three
months of (European) data. Total imports,
annualized, were running at an incredible 2 million a
year pace thanks to strong battery electric imports in

September.

### Page 4

4
China appears to have concluded that it prefers a
guaranteed market for a limited number of cars (and
more or less guaranteed profits) to the risk of a
prohibitive tariff that would cut off all its exports.
And Europe preferred a negotiated deal to the risk of
retaliation and a slowdown in licensing rare earth and
magnet exports.
The Commission also thinks of this as a bit of a
model—more on that later.
I certainly understand the logic of the deal.

Something needed to be done. China’s export of
hybrids to Europe were up an insane 200 percent
year-over-year in the last 3 months of data, which led
to a 75 percent increase for all cars. One more year of
75 percent growth would bring China’s exports to

### Page 5

5
around 3 million cars; in two years, close to 6 million,
and in three years, 10 million—or most of the market.
But Europe still is a long way from a coherent trade
and industrial policy with respect to autos, let alone a
coherent economic policy towards overall trade with
China.
After this deal, as I understand it:
Hybrid imports are capped at 350,000, down
from a projected 800,000 in 2026—and then
rising to 370,000 and 390,000 in subsequent
years.

Battery electric vehicles face a tariff from 25
percent (Telsa, 10 percent base + 15 percent) to
45 percent (10 percent base plus 35 percent).
BYD’s total tariff is at 27 percent, which isn’t
prohibitive. Even with these tariffs, battery
electric imports will total around 600,000 this
year, up from 500,000 last year.
Diesel and gasoline (ICE) cars face a tariff of
10 percent. Europe is importing around
200,000 of these a year (including a significant

number from Chinese-owned MG).
There are no special tariffs on imported parts or
imported batteries, which matters for the growing
number of Chinese factories in Europe. So long as
tariffs are low and the renminbi is undervalued,
Chinese firms still have an incentive to maximize the

### Page 6

6
Chinese content of their “transplant” factories (ask
Thailand).
There is currently no unified European policy on
inward investment that requires joint ventures or
technology transfer either, only a lot of wishful
thinking.**
Sum it up, and it is a market where Chinese exports
are constrained by a hodge-podge of different trade
defense tools, and there is no standard policy for
ensuring that Chinese assembly operations use
European parts rather than import Chinese kits. The

existing operations, which are just starting to scale up,
don’t draw on a European supply chain. They likely
preserve a few assembly jobs at the expense of the
broader automotive ecosystem.
A coherent European (or national) industrial policy
for the automotive and battery sectors is thus still a
work in progress.
The safeguard on hybrids and the resulting tariff rate
quota only stops the bleeding.
And make no mistake, Europe’s position in the global

automotive sector is slipping. Europe still runs an
overall surplus in auto trade, but that surplus is falling
fast if trade in batteries is added to automotive trade
(there isn’t a split between batteries used for energy

### Page 7

7
storage and batteries used by cars in the EU data
right now, at least not that I have found).
Europe’s broader trade policy to China is similarly a
work in progress.
The surge in imports of hybrid cars was particularly
pronounced, but it is by no means the only such surge.
The balance in industrial machinery has eroded
significantly. Consider the following chart, which
shows imports and exports of machinery, electronics
and vehicles (HS 84, 85, and 87) by weight.

### Page 8

8
The balance in chemicals, measured by weight to
abstract from price changes, shows the same trend.
Europe doesn’t have an across-the-board tool for
applying tariffs against China like the U.S. section
301 statute.

### Page 9

9
But that isn’t necessarily an immediate constraint, as
there are plenty of solid safeguard cases thanks to the
current surge in European imports from China.
But if each safeguard needs to include a negotiated
tariff-rate quota that gives China enough guaranteed
market access (and Chinese firms enough guaranteed
profits) to ensure trade peace, Europe needs to get
moving with negotiation in other sectors.
Chemicals and machinery are more diverse sectors

than cars and thus aren’t as amenable to the same
high-level Š ef č ovi č -Wang negotiations the led to the
most recent deal.
This critique isn’t meant as criticism. Europe needed
to act on cars. Look at a chart of automotive trade in
kilos.

### Page 10

10
It’s just that it also needs to act in other sectors: auto
parts, batteries (though that is complicated),
chemicals, and some categories of industrial
machinery. The scale of China’s export boom and the
fact that in most sectors there are no limits to China’s
ability to scale up is a real problem.***
And Europe cannot wait for China to conclude that
it would rather grow on the basis of internal, rather
than external, demand.
I disagree with the IMF’s former top brass on this. A

China that grows on the back of a 1.5 pp net export
contribution for another couple of years will
deindustrialize much of the world. China’s trading
partners cannot step aside and do nothing until
China figures out how to escape deflation using its
own demand. Xi appears more than happy to
continue with his current policy mix and gamble that

### Page 11

11
the world will continue to absorb China’s ever
growing export surplus.
Pascal Lamy, Nicolas Köhler-Suzuki, and Victor Luiz
do Prado of the Jacques Delors Institute recognize
the need for a more systemic response—as do Jean-
Pierre Landau and Sébastien Jean, who have done an
excellent job explaining how China’s micro- and
macro-industrial policies fit together into a coherent,
self-reinforcing whole.
The proposals in the Lamy et al. paper overlap heavily

with the Commission’s current approach to China.
The authors envision a two-pronged approach: first,
negotiating with China to seek agreement on tariff-
rate quotas that effectively limit the EU’s imports
from China; and second, developing a broad yet
targeted tariff-based instrument to address structural
imbalances should negotiations fail. The latter would
give Europe a credible threat should China refuse to
agree to the proposed safeguards.
My own view: don’t let the perfect be the enemy of

the good.
The EU has shied away from developing its own 301
tool out of concern about preserving a “rules-based
system.” I still think a European 301 would allow a
more fulsome response, but understand that
European isn’t as willing as the United States to use

### Page 12

12
tools that at least appear to be outside those
authorized by the WTO. In reality the EU no longer
really wants to trade with China on the same (MFN)
terms applied to other WTO members. That mirrors
the current view of the United States. But the EU is
willing to go through more procedural hoops to get to
a similar result.****
But I also recognize that, absent policy adjustment in
China—notably a stronger currency that allows for
fair market competition—the scope of safeguards and

managed trade will progressively expand.
* One sign: Stellantis is resuming Jeep production in
China, but primarily for export rather than the
domestic market.
** There is a lot of (in my view) somewhat confused
analysis of Chinese investment that works off the
analogy provided by either foreign investment in
China’s auto sector or Japanese investment in the U.S.
and Europe in the 1980s. These are relevant examples
of course, but asserting that Chinese investment will

result in a similar outcome when the underlying
economics differ is a big logical jump. Japanese firms
had incentives to localize their parts production in the
United States from the then-strong yen as well as
from the risk of protection. Foreign firms operating in
China had to do JVs until recently and had incentives
to localize from China’s significant tariff on auto

### Page 13

13
parts and China’s lower cost structure (in part because
of a weak RMB). Tesla’s Shanghai incentives and tax
deal included strong localization incentives, and there
were additional incentives from Tesla’s desire to
qualify for the lists the governed access to China’s
consumer EV subsidies. That is why something like
90 percent of Tesla parts, including its batteries, are
now made in China. Chinese investors in Europe
have no similar incentives to localize right now, yet

many assume that the results will be the same. That,
to me, is naïve. Since the underlying economics differ
(Europe’s cost basis is much higher than existing
production in China) getting Chinese firms to do
more than final assembly in Europe will take stronger
policy tools than were required to get foreign firms to
localize parts production in China. Europe’s
Industrial Accelerator Act would be a step in the
right direction, but more is likely needed. Thailand

provides a useful cautionary example.
*** China can make over 60 million cars, according to
recent reporting from the Wall Street Journal. The
domestic market this year will be under 20 million
cars (down from something like 24 million cars last
year). Even with exports of 10 million, there is ~30
million cars worth of unused auto production
capacity (roughly double European demand). And
China is still investing at a pace that is consistent
with adding close to 5 million of EV production

capacity a year. Chinese firms, who don’t make any
money on domestic sales, are strongly incentivized to

export. The world has never encountered such elastic
supply.
**** The more different the legal basis for the
European and U.S. response—and the more policy
differences (TRQs vs. a flat tariff, for example) the

harder it would be to ever develop a harmonized
policy approach. But with President Trump, a
harmonized policy isn’t even a U.S. goal.

Document 391: Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 1st October 2026

EcoFlash

Threats to European diesel imports: risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased 
risk of price rises

In Europe, the possibility of a US ban on diesel exports has heightened concerns about price trends for this widely used 
fuel1, which has already risen significantly since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran. It has become a major political issue 
on both sides of the Atlantic in the run-up to decisive elections.

A particularly tight diesel market due to a shortage of buffer stocks 
Following the outbreak of the conflict, diesel prices did indeed see some of the sharpest rises on the oil market due to particularly 
tight market conditions. Since the end of February, whilst the price of a barrel of crude has risen by 46%, diesel prices have soared 
by 100% in Europe and by 76% in the United States.

The factors alleviating pressure on the refined products market, and more specifically the diesel market, were more limited than in 
the case of crude oil. Shipments passing through the Strait of Hormuz (around 17% of maritime diesel trade prior to the conflict) 
have fallen by around 75%. Added to this is the halt in Russian exports due to war damage, which is depriving the diesel market of 
around 12% of its maritime exports. Furthermore, unlike with crude oil, China has not acted as a stabilising force in the diesel 
market. On the contrary, the energy crisis has led to a halt in Chinese exports of refined products (including diesel) to the rest of 
Asia from April onwards. The resumption of Chinese exports over the last two months remains fragile, given, in particular, that 
diesel stocks are at their lowest level in 15 months.

The United States has eased these tensions in the diesel market by increasing its exports of refined products (up 25% on average 
in the first half of 2026 compared with the average over the last five years). This rise in exports has required high refinery utilisation 
rate (close to maximum capacity) and, to a lesser extent, a reduction in stocks (-11% compared with the average over the last five 
years as at mid-September for middle distillates2). All these factors have contributed to tightening the US domestic market and 
pushed retail diesel prices to record levels. Road diesel prices (US average) have risen by 68% since the end of February and by 
74% year-on-year.

A structurally high level of vulnerability in Europe… 
Against this market backdrop, characterised by mounting tensions and very limited room for manoeuvre, Europe would be 
particularly hard hit by a reduction in – or even a complete halt to – US exports. According to Eurostat, imports accounted for 46% 
of European demand for diesel in the first half of 2026, with 40% of these imports coming from the United States. Europe, which

1 Diesel accounts for 17% of the primary energy mix in the EU and 7.3% in the United States. 
2 A category of refined petroleum products comprising mainly diesel and aviation fuel.

### Page 2

has lost a great deal of production capacity in recent years, is operating its refineries at near-maximum capacity. Its room for 
manoeuvre is therefore limited.

… But the risk of a shortage has been ruled out in the short term 
The risk of a shortage, however, appears limited in the short term. The situation regarding European stocks is not comfortable, but 
it is not alarming. Commercial diesel stocks in the ARA region3 are currently at historically low levels (around 12 million barrels), 
23% below their level at the end of September 2025. As for emergency stocks (EU Emergency Stocks), these are, according to the 
European Commission, at a sufficient level. Eurostat estimates this figure at 270 million barrels in June 2026 (stable from a year 
earlier), representing approximately 60 days’ worth of EU consumption. Europe imports around 0.9 mb/d of diesel from the United 
States, and emergency stocks would therefore cover around 60 days’ consumption at 4.5 mb/d.

The rise in prices could be significant 
Whatever the outcome of the conflict, diesel prices are likely to continue to rise in the short term. According to our base-case 
scenario, crude oil prices are expected to remain very high in Q4 2026 due to ongoing tensions in the Persian Gulf, whilst a return 
to normal levels of diesel exports from Russia and the Middle East appears unlikely by that time.

Among the factors likely to cause future tensions, the start of the maintenance season in Europe could affect production levels, 
whilst demand for diesel in the United States is typically high in the autumn (agricultural harvests and pre-winter storage). In a 
scenario where tensions remain low, the diesel refining margin (crack spread) could rise by 5% in Q4 compared with its level at the 
end of September 2026, before gradually falling back during 2027. An intermediate scenario (a 10% rise in the crack spread in Q4 
2026) would result in moderate constraints on US diesel exports and a drawdown of European stocks. Finally, an adverse scenario 
(a 35% rise in the crack spread in Q4 2026) would imply severe restrictions on US diesel exports and a delayed European response.

What impact will this have on inflation in the euro area? 
According to our estimates, the rise in diesel prices would exacerbate the expected peak in inflation in the euro area without altering 
its timing. In our baseline scenario for September 2026 (see our recently published EcoPerspective), headline inflation would peak 
at 4.1% y/y in January 2027 before gradually falling back towards 2% by the end of the year. The two least adverse scenarios 
would add only a limited amount to the peak: +0.1 pp in the first scenario (+5% for the crack spread in Q4 compared with the end 
of Q3) and +0.2 pp in the intermediate scenario (+10%), with inflation reaching 4.2% and 4.3% respectively in January 2027. 
However, there is an upside risk in the second scenario linked to the uncertain extent of the spillover effects. In both cases, the 
main impact would be felt through fuel prices (which quickly reflect rising market prices) and would fade over the course of 2027. 
In the case of the adverse scenario (+35%), the additional inflation would be close to 0.6 pp by early 2027, pushing inflation above 
4.6%. A shock of this magnitude would, in fact, reduce businesses’ ability to absorb rising costs, which would lead to price increases 
across a broader range of goods (food, industrial goods). Subsequently, the effect would only fade gradually.

Pascal Devaux 
Economist, Middle East, Western Balkans – Energy 
pascal.devaux@bnpparibas.com

Guillaume Derrien 
Economist, Europe, Eurozone, International Trade 
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com

3 Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp, Europe’s main hub for the storage of raw materials.

Document 398: Top of Mind - Higher Bond Yields_ How Concerning_

Source PDF: all report pdf/Top of Mind - Higher Bond Yields_ How Concerning_.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs (Top of Mind)
Date: 2026-10-19
Pages: 31
Status: ok

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
Global bond yields have risen to multi-decade highs, yet growth and markets have
remained remarkably resilient. But with some pressures beginning to emerge (e.g. in
European sovereign bond markets), can this resilience last, or will more vulnerabilities
be revealed? We ask the IMF’s Tobias Adrian, Moody’s Analytics’ Mark Zandi, and GS
economists. Adrian is relatively sanguine about the economy’s resilience, though
notes downside risks, while GS economists expect only a modest growth drag from

higher rates, but a more consequential one if yields remain near current or higher
levels. Zandi is generally more concerned, describing the US economy as vulnerable
rather than resilient, and sees one-in-three odds of the economy going off the r ails.
Meanwhile, GS strategists and analysts are not especially concerned about banks or
markets more broadly, but warn that further increases in yields or rate volatility could
test risk assets. Key to watch: whether the increasingly interconnected and leveraged

AI investment boom can continue to withstand higher rates.
Tobias Adrian, Special Advisor and former Director of Monetary and Capital Markets, IMF
“The global economy has demonstrated considerable resilience in the face of multiple severe shocks, and I would not be
surprised if it continues to shrug off higher yields, pr ovided they do not keep rising sharply in a disorderly fashion. At the same
time, I do worry about downside risks.”
Mark Zandi, Chief Economist, Moody’s Analytics

We provide a brief snapshot on the most important economies for the global markets

### Page 3

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Recent acceleration in the rise in global bond yields to multi-
decade highs has begun to raise questions about the 
implications for growth and markets. Both have remained 
remarkably resilient so far, with US growth in particular 
holding relatively firm and the S&P 500 and other equity 
markets near new all-time highs. But with pressure 
beginning to emerge—most notably in European sovereign 
bond markets—and yields looking unlikely to retreat soon, 
can that resilience last, or will more vulnerabilities be revealed? 
GS Head of US Rates Strategy William Marshall first lays out 
how we arrived at higher rates, underscoring the striking 
breadth of the rise, which he attributes mainly to 
fundamentals—strong growth, inflation risks, hawkish policy 
shifts, the ongoing investment cycle, and long-run fiscal 
risks—but notes that technical factors likely amplified the 
recent move. And while he ultimately expects yields to fall 
(with YE 10-year US Treasury yields of 4.75%/4.4% in 
2026/2027, respectively) as markets become more confident 
in easing inflation, he thinks elevated long-term yields should 
have some staying power, and sees continued upside risk 
from energy price and fiscal pressures.  
So, just how much of a threat do higher rates pose to the 
macro outlook? For answers, we first turn to Tobias Adrian, 
Special Advisor to the Managing Director and former 
Financial Counsellor and Director of the Monetary and Capital 
Markets Department at the IMF, Mark Zandi, Chief 
Economist of Moody’s Analytics, and GS US economists. 
Adrian sees a global economy that has so far responded to 
the yield shock much as it has to the many other major 
shocks of the past seven years: resiliently. He notes that the 
rise in yields has not significantly tightened financial 
conditions or, more importantly, disrupted market functioning 
or forced damaging deleveraging. He believes that resilience 
could persist as long as the move in yields doesn’t accelerate 
or become more disorderly. But he is concerned about 
vulnerabilities in the AI and technology complex, sovereign 
bond markets, and EMs—not to mention cyber risks—all of 
which leaves him “vigilant but cautiously optimistic.” 
GS US economists also characterize the US economy as 
resilient. Their expectation that the Fed will deliver just one 
more rate hike this year before cutting next year—consistent 
with Marshall’s expectation of lower Treasury yields—leads 
Pierfrancesco Mei to expect only a modest 0.2pp growth 
drag in 2027 from higher rates. But he finds that the drag 
could rise to a more consequential 0.5pp if yields remain near 
current levels (see pg. 9 for GS global economists Joseph 
Briggs’ and Johan Allen’s rules of thumb for the impact of 
higher yields on rate-sensitive areas across major DMs.) 
David Mericle and Mei also worry about the implications for 
the already-concerning US fiscal trajectory, which they 
believe is unlikely to trigger a classic debt crisis but is too risky 
to ignore.     
Zandi, for his part, is generally more concerned. While his 
baseline outlook is relatively sanguine, he describes the US 
economy as vulnerable rather than resilient and views the 
“distribution of possible outcomes as very flat and heavily 
skewed toward darker tail scenarios.” He sees the clearest 
softness in the labor market, with weak hiring and falling real 
wage growth for many workers weighing on consumer 
spending. And he takes little comfort from US growth 
continuing to run around 2%—roughly its potential rate—
noting that AI accounts for roughly one-third of that growth, 
leaving the non-AI economy growing at only around 1-1.5%, 
which he calls “a pretty punk economy.” Zandi also worries 
that the US fiscal situation is “as bad as I have ever seen it” 
and that higher rates are already inflicting damage on real 
estate markets and squeezing existing debtors. As these 
stresses mount, he sees elevated risk of something breaking 
in the financial system. All told, he puts the odds of the 
current vulnerabilities pushing the economy completely off 
the rails at around one-in-three.  
With banks historically at the center of the rate shocks and 
economic crises that Zandi is worried about, we then explore 
how much risk banks pose today. Adrian and GS Head of the 
Financials Equity Group Richard Ramsden both see little 
cause for alarm, explaining that higher rates should boost 
banks’ net interest income and revenues given their asset-
sensitive balance sheets. Ramsden also doesn’t expect a 
significant deterioration in credit quality absent a broader 
economic downturn and says that most banks should have 
excess capital even after accounting for rate-driven portfolio 
losses.   
But even if banks can weather higher rates, what about 
markets more broadly? GS strategists and analysts are not 
especially concerned, with Chief US Equity Strategist Ben 
Snider arguing that current rate levels shouldn’t prevent 
continued strong US earnings growth and the S&P 500 from 
climbing higher, Chief Credit Strategist Amanda Lynam 
viewing the headwind to corporate credit from higher rates 
as manageable amid the solid growth backdrop, and US 
Financials Analyst Alexander Blostein expecting the recent 
slowdown in private market activity to rebound once rates 
stabilize. GS multi-asset strategists Christian Mueller-
Glissmann and Andrea Ferrario add that higher yields have 
strengthened the strategic case for bonds in a multi-asset 
portfolio, though strong earnings continue to favor equities 
for now. But they all warn that if rate volatility remains 
elevated, and especially if yields continue to rise from here, 
risk assets could face mounting pressures.  
All that said, with the AI boom powering the economy and 
markets, everything may come down to whether higher rates 
will derail it. Snider views this as unlikely given the strong 
balance sheets of the firms investing in AI and the expected 
profitability of these investments, with Lynam also seeing a 
high bar to derail AI-related debt issuance. Adrian also 
maintains a positive baseline for the AI economy but is 
concerned about the circularity of investment, increased 
leverage, and the sustainability of revenue growth in the AI 
complex. And Zandi again seems the most worried, warning 
that “AI could become the part of the economy that breaks” 
if rates remain at current levels for much longer.

Higher bond yields: how concerning?

Mark Zandi is Chief Economist of Moody’s Analytics. Below, he argues that the US economy 
is vulnerable rather than resilient and worries that high interest rates could deepen existing 
pockets of weakness and ultimately cause something to break in the financial system.   
The views stated herein are those of the interviewee and do not necessarily reflect those of Goldman Sachs.
Allison Nathan: Prior to the 
September 2026 Fed rate hike, you 
argued that a hike would do little to 
address supply-driven inflation but 
could damage already weak parts of 
the US economy. Where are you 
seeing the most softness? 
Mark Zandi: The clearest softness is 
in the labor market. Monthly payroll 
growth has averaged around 40,000 over the past year and has 
been essentially zero when excluding the healthcare sector. 
Hiring is moribund, hours worked are very low, and while the 
unemployment rate remains low, that is partly a reflection of 
declining labor force participation. Adjusting for that cyclical 
drop would put underlying unemployment in the mid-4% 
range—above the level consistent with full employment—and I 
expect the headline rate to move toward that level over the 
next 6-18 months. Real wage growth is, at best, flat, and for 
many Americans is actually declining, which is eroding 
purchasing power, particularly for lower- and middle-income 
households, who are increasingly drawing down savings to 
sustain spending. So, while the economy is still growing, the 
underlying labor market and household fundamentals point to 
very tenuous growth. 
 While the economy is still growing, the 
underlying labor market and household 
fundamentals point to very tenuous growth.”   
Allison Nathan: What is causing this softness? 
Mark Zandi: Policy choices are at the root of the weakness. 
Tariffs, while more consequential last year, continue to weigh 
on growth and boost inflation. Heavy-handed immigration policy 
is also an underappreciated source of weakness. Many firms 
losing immigrant workers cannot afford to raise wages enough 
to attract native-born workers, so they are raising prices to align 
demand with the supply they can produce with a reduced labor 
force. But the most immediate and significant driver of the 
softness is the Iran war, which has pushed up energy prices, 
further fueling inflation and weighing on growth.  
Allison Nathan: But US real GDP growth remains near 2%—
roughly the economy’s potential rate. Isn’t that a relatively 
healthy growth pace? 
Mark Zandi: Real GDP growth is indeed running at 2% yoy, but 
I estimate that AI-related activity accounts for roughly one-third 
of that, or around 0.6–0.7pp. Roughly half of that comes from AI 
capital spending, net of electronics imports for data centers, 
while the balance reflects wealth effects from the surge in AI-
related stock prices that have contributed to an almost doubling 
in the S&P 500 since ChatGPT debuted four years ago, boosting 
net worth and consumer spending, especially among high-
income households. So, the non-AI economy is growing at only 
around 1%–1.5%. That’s a pretty punk economy, and a difficult 
place to be. 
Allison Nathan: The September SEP indicated that most 
FOMC members expect one more rate hike this year, and 
the market is expecting the same. How damaging could 
further tightening be? 
Mark Zandi: While I expect another hike this year, with the 
economy already barely growing at potential, additional hikes 
could push growth below potential. That would further strain 
the already weak non-AI economy and trigger layoffs, 
significantly raising the likelihood of a self-reinforcing negative 
spiral. So, further tightening would be a risky endeavor. 
 With the economy already barely 
growing at potential, additional hikes could 
push growth below potential. That would 
further strain the already weak non-AI 
economy and trigger layoffs, significantly 
raising the likelihood of a self-reinforcing 
negative spiral.”   
Higher rates are already inflicting damage on the economy’s 
most rate-sensitive sectors, particularly housing and 
commercial real estate. Refinancing is in a deep freeze. Existing 
home sales have fallen to levels last seen during the teeth of 
the housing crash and the Global Financial Crisis. Home prices 
are moving sideways on average and outright declining in half 
the country. That could easily create broader problems for 
households, especially for those that bought homes in the last 
years after rates began to rise and have virtually no home 
equity. And while commercial real estate had begun to show 
signs of life, renewed rate increases are once again freezing 
activity and putting pressure on property values.  
Higher rates are also squeezing existing debtors as borrowing 
costs on credit cards, home loans, auto loans, and corporate 
debt rise. I am particularly worried about highly leveraged, often 
private equity-backed companies that have managed the higher 
rate environment by extending maturities and limiting near-term 
interest payments. That strategy cannot continue indefinitely if 
borrowing costs remain elevated or continue to rise.  
The equity market is another key area of concern. AI continues 
to propel the equity market, but it is not impervious to higher 
rates. Persistently elevated rates will eventually weigh on 
valuations, increasing the risk of a major correction that could 
quickly reverberate through the real economy as positive wealth 
effects reverse and consumer spending weakens. AI capex is 
also vulnerable as a higher cost of capital makes investment 
Interview with Mark Zandi

### Page 5

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 5 
Top of Mind Issue 152

more expensive and falling valuations make it harder to raise 
the capital necessary to sustain the current pace of the AI 
buildout. For now, AI is operating under its own momentum, 
supported by considerable optimism and euphoria. But if rates 
remain at these levels or higher for longer than a quarter or two, 
AI could become the part of the economy that breaks. 
Last but not least, higher rates will worsen the US fiscal 
situation, which is itself contributing to elevated yields today. 
While we have navigated this fiscal risk for some time, the 
fiscal situation is as bad as I have ever seen it. The deficit 
exceeds 6% of GDP, the primary deficit is above 3%, and debt 
is roughly 100% of GDP and on track to reach 125% in a 
decade. Annual federal interest payments are already well 
above $1tn and will rise rapidly if rates remain elevated. All told, 
I see a roughly one-in-five chance of a fiscal cliff event over the 
coming year—unlikely, but large enough to warrant attention. 
So, higher rates will weigh on the economy through several 
channels. Historically, rate increases of this magnitude have 
almost invariably caused something to break in the financial 
system. We cannot know exactly where the fault line will 
emerge, but the risk of these pockets of weakness becoming a 
broader problem is significant. 
 I view the distribution of possible 
outcomes as very flat and heavily skewed 
toward darker tail scenarios.”  
Allison Nathan: How do these risks translate into your 
forecasts for economic growth? 
Mark Zandi: My baseline forecast is relatively sanguine based 
on the assumption that the Iran war begins to wind down, 
allowing oil prices to decline to around $80/bbl as we move into 
next year. That should ease inflation, reduce Fed tightening 
concerns, and bring the 10-year Treasury yield back toward 
4.5% by early next year—a level the economy can digest.  
Importantly, though, I view the distribution of possible 
outcomes as very flat and heavily skewed toward darker tail 
scenarios. The war may not fade into the background, keeping 
energy prices elevated and raising the risk of further inflation 
pressure. That could prompt additional Fed tightening, push 
yields even higher, and ultimately cause something to break in 
the financial system.  
Meanwhile, the Treasury market has grown more fragile as 
traditional, price-insensitive buyers—including the Fed, banks, 
and foreign institutional investors—have stepped back, 
increasing the market’s reliance on highly leveraged, price-
sensitive hedge funds that could rush for the exits under stress. 
A debt-limit confrontation under a likely divided US government 
next year could provide the catalyst, particularly if lawmakers 
underestimate the consequences of a breach. In that scenario, 
an even sharper bond market selloff than we are currently 
experiencing could occur. While none of this is my baseline—
and I remain inherently optimistic because avoiding the darkest 
scenarios is largely a policy choice—I put the odds of a severe 
bond market selloff at around one-in-four and of the economy 
going completely off the rails for any of the reasons we have 
discussed at around one-in-three. So, the downside risks are 
significant. 
 I put the odds of a severe bond market 
selloff at around one-in-four and of the 
economy going completely off the rails for 
any of the reasons we have discussed at 
around one-in-three. So, the downside risks 
are significant.”  
Allison Nathan: The Fed has considerable room to cut 
rates. Should that provide any comfort that the worst-case 
scenarios may be avoided? 
Mark Zandi: That remains an open question. If inflation fades 
and allows the Fed to take its foot off the brake, rate cuts 
should bring yields down across the curve—though not back to 
pre-war levels. But in a darker scenario involving a debt limit 
crisis and even higher yields, short-term rate cuts would have 
little effect on the long end. The Fed would need to turn to 
alternative policy tools, including quantitative easing and bond 
purchases. That would amount to financial repression—a 
strategy similar to the one employed by the BoJ for many 
years—which could prove counterproductive by reducing the 
pressure on lawmakers to address the underlying problems 
while fueling even more persistent inflation. 
Allison Nathan: Why do you think others are more 
sanguine about the risks that higher interest rates pose to 
the US economy? 
Mark Zandi: Some likely see greater underlying economic 
strength and expect AI to drive much stronger growth. Others 
may believe that the neutral rate is higher, perhaps for the 
same reasons, while some may be more concerned about 
persistently higher inflation.  
But the more fundamental difference is the broader lens 
through which I currently view the economy. Many continue to 
describe the US economy as resilient, which I find fascinating, 
bordering on frustrating. In my view, the right word is 
vulnerable. Again, growth is pedestrian, the labor market is soft, 
which was further underscored by the much weaker-than- 
expected September jobs report, real wage growth is 
decelerating, inflation remains elevated, and interest rates are 
rising. That shift in perspective completely changes how one 
evaluates the risks ahead.
